# Meno Observations

## Observation 1

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0001
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 70a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt

greek_terms:
  - διδακτὸν
  - ἀσκητόν
  - φύσει
english_gloss: Meno opens with the question at 70a.
observation: Meno opens the dialogue by asking Socrates whether virtue is teachable, acquired by practice, or comes by nature or some other way.
textual_basis: At 70a, Meno asks whether virtue is teachable, acquired by training/practice, or comes by nature or some other manner.
limits: This is the initiating question of the dialogue. Meno presents the alternatives as a direct question to Socrates.
review_status: accepted
```

## Observation 2

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0002
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 70a-70c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 70a-70c
  start_marker: 70a
  end_marker: 70c
  start_char: 0
  end_char: 1300
  text_sha256: 2fcbef994b4fa1fdfc54097f7c0631d90f6cb98b5e75417803aaa70886ed8e52
greek_terms:
  - Θετταλοὶ
  - Γοργίας
  - Ἀρίστιππος
  - Λαρισαῖοι
english_gloss: Socrates describes Thessalians and Gorgias at 70a-c.
observation: Socrates describes the Thessalians as formerly renowned for horsemanship and wealth, now also for wisdom, with Gorgias responsible for this by attracting students including Aristippus and the Aleuadae, and for teaching the habit of answering boldly.
textual_basis: At 70a-c, Socrates says Thessalians were admired for horsemanship and wealth but now also for wisdom thanks to Gorgias, who drew lovers of wisdom including Aristippus and the leading Aleuadae, and taught them to answer fearlessly and magnificently.
limits: Socrates' description is his own characterization of the Thessalian situation and Gorgias' influence. It is not an objective report.
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 3

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0003
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 71a-71b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 71a-71b
  start_marker: 71a
  end_marker: 71b
  start_char: 920
  end_char: 1748
  text_sha256: af5052f8d3c92bd1ad2d75db1a97fe1cc38f716aa3a747b3df2750c2c1f1bfdb
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: Socrates claims ignorance of what virtue is at 71a-b.
observation: Socrates claims he does not know whether virtue is teachable because he does not even know what virtue itself is — he says he does not know what virtue is at all.
textual_basis: At 71a-b, Socrates says he is so far from knowing whether virtue is teachable that he does not even know what virtue is at all, and another Athenian would say the same, laughing at anyone who thinks an Athenian would know.
limits: Socrates states his own ignorance directly. This is a knowledge-disavowal, the truth of which is tested through the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

## Observation 4

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0004
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 71b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 71b
  start_marker: 71b
  end_marker: 71b
  start_char: 1300
  end_char: 1748
  text_sha256: 9e34c26b0dbf6baffe82f3e4c2bcf7c7ebca872d09eecf9812e92eec04e66bde
greek_terms:
  - ὃ δὲ μὴ οἶδα τί ἐστιν, πῶς ἂν ὁποῖόν γέ τι εἰδείην
english_gloss: Socrates argues one cannot know qualities of the unknown at 71b.
observation: Socrates argues that if he does not know what virtue is, he cannot know what kind of thing it is — just as someone who does not know who Meno is cannot know whether Meno is beautiful, wealthy, or noble.
textual_basis: At 71b, Socrates asks whether someone who does not know Meno at all can know whether Meno is beautiful, wealthy, or noble. Meno agrees this is impossible.
limits: Socrates uses this analogy to argue that knowing the essence must precede knowing attributes. Meno does not dispute the point.
review_status: accepted
```

## Observation 5

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0005

  text_sha256: 4cce6acbc2495028981d9b9d653f613add1be8c5e1bf67d88376f704a004385a
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: Socrates says he never met anyone who knows virtue at 71c.
observation: Socrates claims he has never met anyone who knows what virtue is.
textual_basis: At 71c, when Meno asks whether Socrates really does not know what virtue is, Socrates says not only does he not know, but he thinks he has never met anyone else who knows.
limits: Meno tries to challenge this by mentioning Gorgias, but Socrates avoids the challenge by claiming a poor memory.
review_status: accepted
```

## Observation 6

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0006
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 71e-72a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 71e-72a
  start_marker: 71e
  end_marker: 72a
  start_char: 2501
  end_char: 3419
  text_sha256: 55e9bfb6ce81a5f09bc391b11746e8a9201f05c8e96bcec80c16c8778f92a40a
greek_terms:
  - ἀνδρὸς ἀρετήν
  - γυναικὸς ἀρετήν
  - παιδὸς ἀρετή
  - δούλου
english_gloss: "Meno's first definition: many different virtues at 71e-72a."
observation: "Meno's first definition of virtue lists distinct virtues for different roles: a man's virtue is managing public affairs and helping friends while harming enemies; a woman's virtue is managing the household and being obedient; and there are separate virtues for children, freemen, and slaves."
textual_basis: At 71e-72a, Meno gives separate accounts of virtue for men (managing civic affairs, helping friends, harming enemies), women (managing the home, being obedient), children (boys and girls), elderly, free men, and slaves, concluding there are many virtues.
limits: This is a definition by enumeration of examples rather than a unified account. Socrates will object that he was asking for one virtue, not a swarm of virtues.
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 7

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0007
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 72a-72c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 72a-72c
  start_marker: 72a
  end_marker: 72c
  start_char: 3026
  end_char: 4279
  text_sha256: b3cbe49806ad266896245d227761165c20c310805d3398c817d9933d711bf490
greek_terms:
  - σμῆνός
  - μελίττης
  - εἶδος
english_gloss: Socrates uses the bee/swarm analogy at 72a-c.
observation: Socrates uses a bee/swarm analogy to show Meno that just as bees are many and various but all are bees by the same thing — their bee-being — so many virtues share a single form by which they are virtues.
textual_basis: "At 72a-c, Socrates responds to Meno's list of virtues by saying he has found a swarm of virtues when seeking one. He illustrates with bees: if asked what a bee is, Meno would not list many different kinds but would say what makes them all bees. Meno agrees all bees are the same in being bees."
limits: Meno agrees to the analogy but does not yet grasp how to apply it to virtue. Socrates is using the analogy to clarify what kind of answer he is seeking.
review_status: accepted
```

## Observation 8

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0008
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 72c-73c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 72c-73c
  start_marker: 72c
  end_marker: 73c
  start_char: 3872
  end_char: 6212
  text_sha256: 903771b28492f3dc3f290433b2aee9272eea8bea414b7eb384a29ffa3ccd8b1b
greek_terms:
  - εἶδος
english_gloss: Socrates argues virtues share a single form at 72c-73c.
observation: Socrates argues that all virtues have a single form by which they are virtues, analogous to health, size, and strength being the same form regardless of whether in a man or woman. Meno initially resists but eventually agrees when Socrates shows that being good requires justice and temperance in all cases.
textual_basis: At 72c-e, Socrates says all virtues have one form by which they are virtues. At 72e-73a, Meno initially says virtue is unlike health, size, and strength in this respect. At 73a-c, Socrates shows that both men and women need justice and temperance to be good, leading Meno to agree all humans are good in the same way.
limits: Meno eventually concedes the point through Socratic questioning. The agreement establishes that a single definition of virtue is being sought.
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 9

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0009
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 73c-73d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 73c-73d
  start_marker: 73c
  end_marker: 73d
  start_char: 5812
  end_char: 6631
  text_sha256: 589d27f006fb55497f6f3be32db4806e4662804b1d508c2df7becef0cdc78f5e
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: "Second definition: virtue as ruling over people at 73c-d."
observation: Meno's second definition of virtue is the ability to rule over people, recalling what Gorgias says. Socrates objects that this cannot apply to a child or a slave, who cannot rule their master.
textual_basis: "At 73c-d, when asked what Gorgias says virtue is, Meno answers: the ability to rule over people. Socrates objects that this would not apply to a child or a slave, since a slave would not be ruling his master."
limits: Meno immediately modifies his answer by saying "justly, not unjustly." Socrates then pursues the distinction between virtue and a virtue.
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 10

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0010
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 73d-73e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 73d-73e
  start_marker: 73d
  end_marker: 73e
  start_char: 6212
  end_char: 6979
  text_sha256: 4242ecfe16654164f14d3a373fd2e0718f1a7aa9317e1448694ff6d0b0dade51
greek_terms:
  - ἀρετή
  - ἀρετή τις
  - δικαιοσύνη
english_gloss: Socrates distinguishes virtue from a virtue at 73d-e.
observation: When Meno says justice is virtue, Socrates distinguishes between "virtue" and "a virtue," using the analogy of roundness being a shape but not simply shape (since there are other shapes). Meno agrees and says there are other virtues besides justice.
textual_basis: At 73d-e, Meno says justice is virtue. Socrates asks if it is virtue or a virtue, explaining that roundness is a shape, not simply shape, because there are other shapes. Meno agrees and says there are other virtues besides justice.
limits: Socrates is establishing a genus/species distinction. Meno follows the analogy but has not yet provided a definition at the genus level.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
  - σωφροσύνη
  - σοφία
  - μεγαλοπρέπεια
english_gloss: Meno lists virtue names at 73e-74a.
observation: When asked for other virtues, Meno lists courage, temperance, wisdom, magnificence, and many others. Socrates responds that they have again found many virtues when seeking one.
textual_basis: At 73e-74a, Socrates asks Meno to list other virtues besides justice. Meno names courage, temperance, wisdom, magnificence, and many others. Socrates says they have found many virtues again, though in a different way than before.
limits: Meno continues to list examples rather than giving the single form they all share. Socrates notes they have not yet found the one virtue that runs through them all.
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 12

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0012
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 74a-75a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 74a-75a
  start_marker: 74a
  end_marker: 75a
  start_char: 6979
  end_char: 9566
  text_sha256: ca6fd85b090806dae8ecf84ea895fd9f2525c6853a632262517fc227802d1fdb
greek_terms:
  - τὸ ἐπὶ πᾶσιν τούτοις ταὐτόν
  - σχῆμα
  - στρογγύλον
  - εὐθύ
english_gloss: Socrates uses shape and color to teach definition by common form at 74a-75a.
observation: Socrates uses shape and color as examples to teach Meno the method of defining by the common element across all instances, distinguishing between instances (round, straight) and the universal (shape itself). He asks what is the same across round and straight that is called shape.
textual_basis: At 74a-75a, Socrates explains that if asked what shape is, one should not list round and straight separately but find what is the same in all shapes. He asks what is the same in round and straight and all things called shapes.
limits: Meno cannot answer this himself and asks Socrates to provide the definition of shape, which Socrates then does.
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 13

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0013
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 75a-75d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 75a-75d
  start_marker: 75a
  end_marker: 75d
  start_char: 9072
  end_char: 10762
  text_sha256: 5c02864d99d214345430b7eefcfeabf5a3c669b25143c0a6beee1749d3422f59
greek_terms:
  - χρώματι ἀεὶ ἑπόμενον
  - διαλεκτικώτερον
english_gloss: Socrates defines shape as what always follows color at 75b-75d.
observation: Socrates proposes that shape is the only thing among beings that always accompanies color. Meno rejects this as foolish, objecting that if someone does not know color, the definition would be useless. Socrates then distinguishes between eristic and friendly/dialectical modes of answering.
textual_basis: At 75b-c, Socrates defines shape as what alone always follows color. Meno objects that this is foolish because if someone does not know color either, the definition fails. At 75c-d, Socrates distinguishes between eristic disputers (to whom one says what one thinks and challenges them to refute it) and friendly dialectic (where one answers through what the questioner agrees they know).
limits: Meno rejects the first definition of shape for circularity. Socrates agrees and offers a better one.
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 14

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0014
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 75e-76a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 75e-76a
  start_marker: 75e
  end_marker: 76a
  start_char: 10762
  end_char: 11493
  text_sha256: 0302f882b2e325b66a191e9b1abd1c33c377bec282b396f28c8d3e28807a31f4
greek_terms:
  - πέρας
  - ἐπίπεδον
  - στερεόν
  - στερεοῦ πέρας σχῆμα
english_gloss: Socrates defines shape as the limit of a solid at 75e-76a.
observation: Socrates defines shape by first establishing that Meno agrees on the concepts of limit/end and plane/solid, then defines shape as "the limit of a solid." Meno accepts this definition.
textual_basis: At 75e-76a, Socrates asks whether Meno calls something "end" or "limit" and "plane" and "solid," then defines shape as "that into which the solid ends" — i.e., the limit of a solid. Meno accepts this.
limits: Meno quickly moves on to ask about color rather than proceeding to virtue, delaying the return to the definition of virtue. Socrates criticizes him for this.
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 15

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0015
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 76a-77a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 76a-77a
  start_marker: 76a
  end_marker: 77a
  start_char: 11053
  end_char: 13516
  text_sha256: c2f6eed2a5982c5a0895c6fb2cb52ef12ebf8eaf218321f606bb88c06fad12e0
greek_terms:
  - ἀπορροάς
  - Ἐμπεδοκλέα
  - χρόα
  - ὄψει σύμμετρος
english_gloss: Socrates defines color via Empedoclean theory at 76a-d.
observation: 'Socrates defines color using Empedoclean terminology: color is an effluence of figures, commensurate with sight, and perceptible. He prefaces this by saying Meno is spoiled (having lovers) and demands answers like a tyrant, so Socrates will indulge him. Meno prefers this definition, calling it excellent, but Socrates calls it "tragic" and says the shape definition was better.'
textual_basis: "At 76a-d, Socrates says he will answer Meno's demand and give the definition of color following Gorgias/Empedocles: color is an effluence of shapes commensurate with sight and perceptible. At 76e-77a, Meno strongly praises this answer, but Socrates says the definition of shape was actually better, calling his color definition \"tragic.\""
limits: Socrates explicitly values the shape definition more than the color definition, though Meno prefers the latter. This is an explicit comparison by Socrates within the text.
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 16

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0016
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 77a-78b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 77a-78b
  start_marker: 77a
  end_marker: 78b
  start_char: 13052
  end_char: 15892
  text_sha256: 115b35c2bb796bfee1db0ecd464cadc20cc7dd73f0004da5c2d234711f9123a6
greek_terms:
  - χαίρειν τε καλοῖσι καὶ δύνασθαι
  - ἐπιθυμοῦντα τῶν καλῶν δυνατὸν εἶναι πορίζεσθαι
english_gloss: "Third definition: virtue is desiring fine things and being able to get them at 77a-b."
observation: "Meno's third definition of virtue, following the poet, is \"to desire beautiful things and be able to procure them.\" Socrates examines this: if beautiful things are good, then everyone desires good things, so the desire part applies to all. He argues that no one desires bad things, either because they think bad things are good or because they know they are bad but do not actually want to be wretched. Therefore, the definition reduces to the ability part."
textual_basis: "At 77a-b, Meno defines virtue as desiring beautiful things and being able to procure them. At 77b-78b, Socrates refines: beautiful = good. Some desire bad things thinking them good; those who know bad things are bad do not actually want them (since no one wants to be wretched). So everyone desires good things, making desire universal. Therefore being able to procure good things is what distinguishes people."
limits: Socrates does not raise this as a final objection. He proceeds to examine what "procuring good things" means and what counts as good things.
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 17

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0017
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 78b-79c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 78b-79c
  start_marker: 78b
  end_marker: 79c
  start_char: 15463
  end_char: 18554
  text_sha256: 71ef3fff4b0cb008a9c89a06b73cd33a08d0e81525cc1a2bdb79903b6bec3e9c
greek_terms:
  - δύναμις τοῦ πορίζεσθαι τἀγαθά
  - δικαίως
  - ὁσίως
  - μόριον
english_gloss: Virtue defined as ability to procure goods; justice problem at 78b-79c.
observation: Socrates refines the definition to "virtue is the ability to procure good things." Meno lists goods as health, wealth, gold, silver, honors, and political offices. Socrates then asks whether procuring these justly and piously is virtue, or if unjust procurement also counts. Meno says only just/righteous procurement is virtue, and justice is a part of virtue. Socrates objects that this defines the whole through its parts, which is circular.
textual_basis: "At 78b-c, Socrates states Meno's refined definition: virtue is the power/ability to get good things. At 78c-d, Meno specifies goods include health, wealth, gold, silver, honors, offices. At 78d-79a, Socrates asks about doing so justly and piously, and Meno agrees these are needed. At 79a-c, Socrates notes that justice is a part of virtue, so defining virtue as acting with a part of virtue is circular."
limits: The objection is that the definition presupposes knowledge of virtue's parts, which requires prior knowledge of virtue itself.
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 18

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0018
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 79c-79e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 79c-79e
  start_marker: 79c

  start_char: 18053
  end_char: 19246
  text_sha256: b15b53223c43221ec218998312831e93b0a474fd65f3edfda0423d2ab61b0d05
greek_terms:
  - κατακερματίζῃς
  - μόρια
  - ἐξ ἀρχῆς
english_gloss: Socrates rejects defining whole through undefined parts at 79c-e.
observation: "Socrates explicitly rejects defining virtue by its parts when the whole is still being investigated, comparing this to his earlier rejection of defining shape through what is still being investigated. He insists on returning to the starting question: what is virtue itself."
textual_basis: At 79c-e, Socrates says defining virtue as action with a part of virtue assumes knowledge of the whole. He recalls rejecting the same approach when defining shape — answering through things still being investigated. He asks Meno to answer again from the beginning what virtue is.
limits: Socrates' methodological objection is explicit in the text. Meno concedes the point.
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 19

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0019
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 79e-80d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 79e-80d
  start_marker: 79e
  end_marker: 80d
  start_char: 18953
  end_char: 21011
  text_sha256: 0f1feaeec42f3c2c8908932d75a784dd769ff1bd4a544a27e7a1ad04c2b54977
greek_terms:
  - ἀπορεῖς
  - γοητεύεις
  - φαρμάττεις
  - νάρκῃ
  - ναρκᾶν
english_gloss: Meno compares Socrates to a torpedo fish at 80a-d.
observation: Meno says he has heard before meeting Socrates that Socrates causes others to be at a loss, and now Socrates is bewitching and drugging him so he is full of aporia. Meno compares Socrates to a flat torpedo fish that numbs anyone who touches it — his mind and mouth are numb and he cannot answer what virtue is, though he has spoken about it many times before.
textual_basis: At 79e-80b, Meno says Socrates does nothing but cause aporia in himself and others, and now Socrates has numbed him like a torpedo fish — his soul and mouth are numb. At 80b-d, Meno continues that he has made many speeches about virtue before but now cannot say what it is.
limits: "Meno states his own experience of aporia. Socrates' response distinguishes himself from the torpedo fish: he causes aporia in others because he himself is in aporia, not because he has resources."
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 20

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0020
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 80d-81e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 80d-81e
  start_marker: 80d
  end_marker: 81e
  start_char: 20596
  end_char: 23305
  text_sha256: abe71f5b6738b0c68781f26cc5d92d189fc732b84154bc9cb6d90dc6eb52144b
greek_terms:
  - ἐριστικὸν λόγον
  - ἀθάνατον
  - Πίνδαρος
  - Φερσεφόνα
english_gloss: The theory of recollection from priests and Pindar at 80d-81e.
observation: Socrates responds to Meno's paradox (one cannot search for what one does not know) by citing priests, priestesses, and Pindar who say the soul is immortal, undergoes rebirth, has seen everything, and therefore can recollect what it once knew — so learning is recollection. He argues this makes people energetic and investigative rather than lazy.
textual_basis: "At 80d-e, Meno presents the eristic argument that one cannot search for what one does not know (since one would not know what to look for) nor for what one knows (since one already knows it). At 80e-81a, Socrates rejects this. At 81a-e, he gives the counter-argument from priests, Pindar, and divine poets: the soul is immortal, is reborn, has seen all things, so learning is recollection of what the soul already knows."
limits: Socrates attributes the theory to religious and poetic sources, not to himself as an originator. He later says he would not insist on the whole argument but would fight for the practical conclusion that one should search.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
  start_char: 22998
  end_char: 23679
  text_sha256: 83dc3593ddf94d1071c968db20fb604a9611170886d27cb43e2eb81f5a70cd38
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: Socrates avoids teaching to avoid contradiction at 81e-82a.
observation: When Meno asks Socrates to teach him how learning is recollection, Socrates accuses Meno of trying to make him contradict himself, since he denies teaching exists and claims only recollection. He says he cannot teach, but can demonstrate by showing.
textual_basis: At 81e-82a, Meno asks Socrates to teach him that learning is recollection. Socrates says Meno is being tricky, since he claims there is no teaching but only recollection, so if he taught, he would contradict himself. He offers to give a demonstration instead, calling a slave boy to question.
limits: Socrates explicitly avoids the word "teach" for the upcoming demonstration, though he will demonstrate the process of recollection through questioning.
review_status: accepted
```

## Observation 22

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0022
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 82a-85b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 82a-85b
  start_marker: 82a
  end_marker: 85b
  start_char: 23305
  end_char: 30330
  text_sha256: fa00a8e52a742e4581a805a08f6d77cdf1ba684146462ce5d9cdcc1e62059487
greek_terms:
  - τετράγωνον
  - ὀκτώπουν
  - διπλάσιον
  - διάμετρος
english_gloss: Socrates questions a slave boy about geometry at 82a-85b.
observation: "Socrates questions a slave boy (one of Meno's attendants) about how to construct a square with double the area of a given square. Initially the boy confidently gives wrong answers (double the side, then a side of three feet), is reduced to aporia realizing he does not know, and then through further questioning arrives at the correct answer: the diagonal of the original square."
textual_basis: "At 82a-85b, Socrates questions the slave boy step by step: establishing a 2-foot square (4 sq ft), asking about a square double the size (8 sq ft). The boy first says the side must be double (4 ft), then tries 3 ft, realizes that gives 9 sq ft, not 8. He is reduced to aporia (84a). Then through further questioning about diagonals, he agrees the square on the diagonal of the 2-ft square gives exactly 8 sq ft."
limits: Socrates asks questions throughout and claims he is not teaching but drawing out the boy's own opinions. The demonstration is presented as evidence for the recollection theory.
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 23

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0023
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 85b-86b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 85b-86b
  start_marker: 85b
  end_marker: 86b
  start_char: 29915
  end_char: 32504
  text_sha256: 708b89f802aa9d640f3a684d83d4f9508e751414f33e6624dadeaf7ef94e8fef
greek_terms:
  - ἀληθεῖς δόξαι
english_gloss: Socrates argues true opinions were innate in the slave at 85b-86b.
observation: Socrates argues that since the slave boy was never taught geometry but gave correct answers from his own opinions, these true opinions were in him all along. He concludes they were not acquired in this life, so the soul must have learned them before birth, supporting the immortality of the soul and the doctrine that learning is recollection.
textual_basis: At 85b-86b, Socrates asks whether the slave gave any answer that was not his own opinion; Meno says no. Socrates notes the boy did not know but had true opinions stirred up like a dream. If asked repeatedly, he would gain exact knowledge. Since no one taught him in this life, the knowledge was acquired earlier, when the soul was not yet human. Therefore the soul is immortal and learning is recollection.
limits: Socrates later says he would not insist on every detail of the argument but would strongly defend the practical conclusion that one should inquire. Meno accepts the conclusion with reservations ("I think you speak well, I don't know how").
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 24

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0024
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 86b-86d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 86b-86d
  start_marker: 86b
  end_marker: 86d
  start_char: 32013
  end_char: 33382
  text_sha256: d8c5085dbc77330aafc1eff57965b7457753f6bd79a4397e4176345f01799711
greek_terms:
  - ζητητέον
english_gloss: Socrates and Meno agree to investigate together at 86b-d.
observation: Socrates and Meno agree that they should inquire into what virtue is. However, Meno insists on first investigating whether virtue is teachable, as he originally asked. Socrates reluctantly concedes to Meno's procedural demand, saying Meno wants to be free and rules Socrates.
textual_basis: "At 86b-c, Socrates says they should inquire together what virtue is. At 86c-d, Meno insists on first returning to his original question about teachability. Socrates concedes but notes that if he were in charge they would first investigate what virtue is before asking about its properties, and he asks for a small concession: to examine it by hypothesis."
limits: Socrates explicitly states the methodological priority — essence before properties — but yields to Meno's demand. This is a clear textual instance of procedural negotiation.
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 25

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0025

stephanus_span: 86e-87b

  start_char: 33382
  end_char: 34584
  text_sha256: f7375758fb12bf0097ce4404e34ce1bcccdacc5a8da6e4a4e90964bced5e5b90
greek_terms:
  - ἐξ ὑποθέσεως
  - ὥσπερ οἱ γεωμέτραι
english_gloss: Socrates proposes investigating by hypothesis like geometers at 86e-87b.
observation: Socrates proposes investigating whether virtue is teachable by hypothesis, using the method geometers use when asked whether a given area can be inscribed as a triangle in a given circle — they hypothesize a condition and see what follows. He proposes hypothesizing what kind of thing virtue is among things related to the soul.
textual_basis: "At 86e-87b, Socrates says he will investigate by hypothesis, like geometers: if asked whether a triangle can be inscribed in a circle, they hypothesize a condition about the area and see what follows. Similarly, he will hypothesize about virtue and see whether it follows that it is teachable."
limits: The hypothesis method is presented as a substitute for direct definition. Socrates uses it because Meno will not let him investigate essence first.
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 26

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0026

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt

  end_char: 35445
  text_sha256: bf8724b7c0907db435f87c3106995376570a9fe7009ee539227bbd225f28693a
greek_terms:
  - ἐπιστήμη
  - διδακτόν
english_gloss: "Hypothesis: if virtue is knowledge, it is teachable at 87b-d."
observation: "Socrates sets up the hypothesis: if virtue is a kind of knowledge, then it is teachable (since only knowledge is taught). If virtue is not a kind of knowledge, it is not teachable. The next step will be to examine whether virtue is knowledge."
textual_basis: At 87b-c, Socrates says it is clear that nothing other than knowledge is taught to a human. So if virtue is knowledge, it is teachable; if it is something else, it is not. At 87d, he says they need to investigate whether virtue is knowledge or something different from knowledge.
limits: The hypothesis depends on the premise that only knowledge is teachable. Socrates does not argue for this premise at this point.
review_status: accepted
```

## Observation 27

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0027

  end_char: 38032
  text_sha256: 6f80594837c9b142a4ac037b42706fa310f8ae84f871bda6eea6c0ce22f04d6e
greek_terms:
  - ὠφέλιμον
  - φρόνησις
  - σωφροσύνη
  - δικαιοσύνη
english_gloss: Socrates argues virtue is beneficial and therefore wisdom at 87d-89a.
observation: Socrates argues that virtue is good and beneficial. Examining what is beneficial, he shows that external goods (health, wealth, etc.) and even soul-qualities (courage, temperance) benefit only when guided by wisdom/phronesis and harm when guided by folly. Therefore virtue, being beneficial, must be wisdom/phronesis.
textual_basis: "At 87d-89a, Socrates argues: virtue is good and beneficial. External goods (health, wealth) benefit when used rightly and harm when not. Soul-qualities (temperance, courage, learning, memory) also benefit only when guided by wisdom and harm when guided by folly. Therefore virtue, as beneficial, must be a kind of wisdom."
limits: The argument relies on the premise that virtue is good and beneficial, which Meno accepts. The conclusion that virtue is wisdom follows from these premises.
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 28

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0028
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 89a-89c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 89a-89c
  start_marker: 89a
  end_marker: 89c
  start_char: 37710
  end_char: 38847
  text_sha256: cf01c5ac2228da4e78e29221282d49c41829710d0edeadfc1ca0e046170b1249
greek_terms:
  - οὐ φύσει
english_gloss: Socrates argues good men are not good by nature at 89a-c.
observation: From the conclusion that virtue is wisdom, Socrates infers that good men are not good by nature — because if they were, there would be people who recognize good natures in the young and would guard them in the acropolis like gold. Since there is no such practice, good men are not good by nature. Therefore they must be good through learning.
textual_basis: At 89a-c, Socrates says if virtue is wisdom, good men cannot be good by nature. If they were, there would be experts who identify good natures and guard them, which does not happen. Therefore good men must be good through learning — i.e., virtue is teachable.
limits: The argument depends on the previous conclusion (virtue = wisdom) and the premise that there are no recognized identifiers of natural good natures. Socrates himself will soon express doubt about the whole chain.
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 29

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0029

greek_terms:
  - ἀπιστεῖς
english_gloss: Socrates expresses doubt about virtue being knowledge at 89c-e.
observation: Immediately after agreeing that virtue is teachable (if it is knowledge), Socrates expresses doubt, saying he may not have been right to agree. Meno is surprised, noting the reasoning seemed correct. Socrates says it must seem correct not only just now but also later if it is to be sound.
textual_basis: 'At 89c-e, Socrates says "perhaps by Zeus this was not rightly agreed to." Meno asks why Socrates is suspicious and doubts that virtue is knowledge. Socrates says he will explain: if there are teachers of virtue, it is teachable; if there are no teachers, it is not.'
limits: Socrates shifts the criterion from hypothetical reasoning to empirical investigation — whether there are actual teachers of virtue.
review_status: accepted
```

## Observation 30

```yaml
greek_terms:
  - διδάσκαλοι
  - Ἄνυτος
english_gloss: Socrates introduces Anytus into the discussion at 89e-90a.
observation: Socrates says he cannot find teachers of virtue despite searching. He notes that Anytus has just sat down beside them and proposes to include him in the search, since Anytus is the son of a wealthy and wise father (Anthemion) who became wealthy through his own wisdom and industry, and Anytus has been chosen for the highest offices by the Athenians.
textual_basis: At 89e-90a, Socrates says he has tried to find teachers of virtue but cannot. He then notes Anytus has just sat down and proposes including him. Socrates describes Anytus' father Anthemion as having acquired wealth through wisdom and effort, and Anytus as having been well-raised and chosen for the highest offices.
limits: Socrates' description of Anytus and Anthemion is part of the dialogue's framing. Anytus was a historical figure known for being a prosecutor of Socrates.
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 31

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0031

greek_terms:
  - ἰατρούς
  - σκυτοτόμους
english_gloss: Socrates uses craft analogy for finding teachers at 90a-e.
observation: "Socrates asks Anytus: if Meno wanted to become a good doctor, one would send him to doctors; to become a good shoemaker, to shoemakers; and only to those who claim to be teachers and charge fees. Anytus agrees this is the correct method for finding teachers of any craft."
textual_basis: At 90a-e, Socrates systematically asks Anytus about sending someone to learn medicine (from doctors), shoemaking (from shoemakers), and auletic (from auletic teachers), establishing that one should send to those who claim to teach the craft and charge fees. Anytus agrees throughout.
limits: "This establishes a criterion for identifying teachers: those who profess to teach and charge fees. Anytus will reject this criterion when applied to virtue."
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 32

```yaml
greek_terms:
  - σοφιστάς
english_gloss: Socrates suggests sophists as teachers of virtue at 91a-c.
observation: When Socrates asks who teaches political virtue (managing households and cities, caring for parents, hosting citizens and strangers), Meno says it would be those who claim to be teachers of virtue — the sophists. Anytus reacts with horror, saying "Heracles! Hush!" and denounces sophists as manifest ruin and corruption.
textual_basis: At 91a-b, Meno says Meno should be sent to those who claim to be teachers of virtue and charge fees. Socrates asks who they are; Meno says the sophists. At 91c, Anytus says "Heracles, hush!" and says no one he knows should be afflicted with such madness as to go to sophists, who are manifest ruin and corruption.
limits: Anytus' strong condemnation of sophists is stated directly. Socrates questions this judgment.
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 33

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0033

greek_terms:
  - Πρωταγόραν
  - Φειδίαν
english_gloss: Socrates defends Protagoras against Anytus at 91c-92c.
observation: Socrates defends the sophists by noting that Protagoras earned more money from his wisdom than the renowned sculptor Pheidias and ten other statue-makers, and remained highly esteemed for over 40 years. He argues that if sophists really corrupted the young, they could not have gone undetected for so long. Anytus insists he knows sophists are harmful without having met any.
textual_basis: At 91d-e, Socrates says Protagoras earned more money than Pheidias (the famous sculptor) and others, and was esteemed for over 40 years across Greece. At 92a, Socrates asks whether the sophists knowingly corrupt the young or are themselves deceived. At 92b, Anytus says he has never met a sophist. At 92c, Socrates questions how Anytus can judge them without experience.
limits: Anytus admits he has no direct experience with sophists but claims to know what they are like. Socrates questions the basis of this knowledge.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation: When Socrates asks Anytus who can teach virtue among the Athenians, Anytus says any noble/good Athenian (kalos kagathos) Meno encounters will make him better than the sophists would. Socrates questions whether these gentlemen became good spontaneously without learning from anyone, yet can teach what they themselves never learned.
textual_basis: At 92e, Anytus says any noble and good Athenian Meno encounters will improve him more than the sophists, if he is willing to obey. At 92e-93a, Socrates asks whether these gentlemen became good spontaneously without learning from anyone, yet are able to teach what they themselves never learned.
limits: Anytus' claim is that Athenian gentlemen are teachers of virtue simply by association. Socrates questions the logical coherence of this claim.
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 35

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0035

greek_terms:
  - Θεμιστοκλέα

  - Ἀριστείδην
  - Λυσίμαχον
  - Περικλέα
  - Πάραλον
  - Ξάνθιππον
english_gloss: Socrates examines famous Athenian statesmen's sons at 93a-94e.
observation: "Socrates examines four famous Athenian statesmen: Themistocles (his son Cleophantus was trained in horsemanship but not in his father's virtue), Aristides (son Lysimachus was not virtuous), Pericles (sons Paralus and Xanthippus were well-trained in arts but not in virtue), and Thucydides (sons Melesias and Stephanus were trained in wrestling but not in virtue). All gave their sons excellent training in teachable skills but none could make them virtuous, suggesting virtue is not teachable."
textual_basis: "At 93a-94e, Socrates goes through each statesman: Themistocles taught his son Cleophantus to be an excellent horseman but not a good man (93c-e); Aristides' son Lysimachus was not noteworthy (94a); Pericles taught his sons Paralus and Xanthippus music, athletics, and arts but not virtue (94b-c); Thucydides taught his sons Melesias and Stephanus to be the best wrestlers but not good men (94c-d). Anytus agrees with each case."
limits: The argument assumes the statesmen wanted their sons to be virtuous and would have enabled it if possible. Anytus confirms each case but does not contest the interpretation.
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 37

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0037

greek_terms:
  - Θέογνιν
english_gloss: Theognis quoted as contradicting himself on teachability at 95d-96a.
observation: Socrates quotes the poet Theognis, who in one passage says that you learn good things from good people, and in another says that virtue cannot be implanted or taught. Since even the poets contradict themselves, there is no consistent authority on whether virtue is teachable. Neither sophists nor gentlemen agree among themselves.
textual_basis: 'At 95d-96a, Socrates quotes Theognis: "from good men you will learn good things, and if you associate with bad you will destroy even the mind you have" (95e), then "if it were possible to implant understanding... never from a good father would a bad son come... by teaching you will never make a bad man good" (95e-96a). At 96a-b, Socrates notes the poets and the gentlemen contradict themselves.'
limits: The contradictory quotes from Theognis are directly cited. Socrates uses this as evidence of confusion about whether virtue is teachable.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 96b-c, Socrates argues: if neither sophists nor gentlemen are teachers of virtue, there are no teachers. If there are no teachers, there are no students. If there are neither teachers nor students, the subject is not teachable. ## Observation 39
```

```yaml

  - ὀρθὴ δόξα
  - φρόνησις
  - Λάρισαν
english_gloss: Socrates introduces right opinion as guide to action at 96e-97c.
observation: "Socrates realizes they made an error in assuming only wisdom/knowledge can guide action correctly. He uses the example of the road to Larissa: someone who has correct opinion about the road (but has not traveled it) can guide others just as well as someone who knows the road from experience. Therefore true opinion is as good a guide as knowledge for correct action."
textual_basis: "At 96e-97c, Socrates says they have been foolishly missing something: both wisdom/knowledge and true opinion can guide action correctly. The road to Larissa example: someone who has correct opinion about which road to take (without having traveled it) can guide as well as someone who knows from experience. Thus true opinion is no worse a guide to correct action than knowledge."
limits: Meno objects that knowledge is more stable since the one with knowledge always succeeds while the one with true opinion succeeds only sometimes. Socrates will address this stability issue.
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 40

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0040
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 97d-98a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 97d-98a

  start_char: 54902
  end_char: 56143
  text_sha256: 28871ec222630ed58422db6edeaf1eab3408027060d9048f3019a2ee304cec44
greek_terms:
  - Δαιδάλου
  - δήσῃ αἰτίας λογισμῷ
  - ἀνάμνησις
english_gloss: Daedalus analogy for true opinions at 97d-98a.
observation: Socrates compares true opinions to the statues of Daedalus — they are beautiful but run away unless tied down. True opinions are valuable while they stay, but they do not remain long in the soul. They become stable knowledge only when bound by reasoning about the cause (aitias logismos), which is recollection/learning as previously established.
textual_basis: At 97d-98a, Socrates says Daedalus' statues run away if not tied down; true opinions are like that — they are good while they remain but run away from the soul. When bound by reasoning about the cause, they become knowledge and become stable. This binding is recollection.
limits: Socrates says he speaks as one who does not know but conjectures. He says he would claim to know that true opinion differs from knowledge, which is one of the few things he would claim to know.
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 41

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0041
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 98a-99a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 98a-99a
  start_marker: 98a
  end_marker: 99a
  start_char: 55672
  end_char: 58136
  text_sha256: c5c5895c90469253ef0f6e2a593e099d7ee121c77af51a4045c6582b353b2b8b
greek_terms:
  - ἐπιστήμη
  - ὀρθὴ δόξα
english_gloss: True opinion and knowledge distinguished at 98a-99a.
observation: Socrates argues that since virtue is not teachable (no teachers), it is not knowledge. Therefore virtue must be true opinion, since both knowledge and true opinion guide correct action. Neither knowledge nor true opinion is present by nature — they are acquired. Since virtue is not knowledge and not by nature, good men cannot produce others like themselves.
textual_basis: "At 98a-99a, Socrates reviews: virtue is beneficial; only true opinion and knowledge guide correctly; virtue is not teachable, so it is not knowledge; therefore it must be true opinion. Neither is present by nature. Since it is not by nature, good men are not good by nature."
limits: The argument relies on the previous conclusion that there are no teachers of virtue. The conclusion about divine dispensation follows from this.
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 42

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0042
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 99a-99d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 99a-99d
  start_marker: 99a
  end_marker: 99d
  start_char: 57755
  end_char: 59533
  text_sha256: f5367dfc7a2c995ffdbd82e4c626803e27fa24fbed0bb3b5ad3ab38a39c6132f
greek_terms:
  - χρησμῳδοί
  - θεομάντεις
  - ἐνθουσιῶντες
english_gloss: Virtue as divine dispensation at 99a-d.
observation: Socrates compares good political men to oracle-singers and soothsayers who say many true things without knowledge, inspired by the god. Since statesmen like Themistocles and those Anytus mentioned guide cities correctly by true opinion without knowledge, they are divine and inspired, acting by divine dispensation rather than by wisdom.
textual_basis: At 99a-c, Socrates says if virtue is not knowledge, then political men guide cities by true opinion, like oracle-singers and divine prophets who speak many true things but know nothing of what they say. At 99c-d, he says they should be called divine and inspired, possessed by the god when they succeed in great matters without knowledge.
limits: The comparison of statesmen to diviners and soothsayers is drawn explicitly by Socrates. Meno agrees with this characterization.
review_status: rejected
```

## Observation 43

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0043
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 99e-100c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 99e-100c
  start_marker: 99e
  end_marker: 100c
  start_char: 59533
  end_char: 60738
  text_sha256: bee05cbd90cb8a29a1e709925f0c1c3fa23034be35691e757ac468f62379a6ab
greek_terms:
  - θείᾳ μοίρᾳ
  - Τειρεσίαν
  - σκιαὶ
  - ὥρα ποι ἰέναι
english_gloss: "Conclusion: virtue by divine dispensation; need to ask what it is at 99e-100c."
observation: Socrates concludes that virtue comes to those who have it by divine dispensation without understanding, unless someone is able to make another person a political man. Such a person would be like Tiresias among the dead — a real thing among shadows. He then says it is time for him to go, but insists Meno should convince Anytus of these things, and that they will know clearly when they first investigate what virtue is itself before asking how it comes to people.
textual_basis: "At 99e-100b, Socrates concludes: if they have inquired correctly, virtue comes by divine dispensation without understanding to those who have it, unless someone can make another political. Such a person would be like the solid reality Homer says Tiresias is among the shades. At 100b-c, Socrates says it is time for him to go; they will know clearly when they investigate what virtue itself is before asking how it comes."
limits: The dialogue ends without a full definition of virtue. Socrates states the final conclusion as conditional ("if we have inquired correctly") and points to future inquiry as necessary for clear knowledge.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0044
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 71c-71d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 71c-71d
  start_marker: 71c
  end_marker: 71d
  start_char: 1748
  end_char: 2501
  text_sha256: 4ec9f960f76c79d2a6f2120cba7ee69021a42ab8f4c1923eb9fae6d751f1cc1a
greek_terms:
  - οὐ πάνυ εἰμὶ μνήμων
english_gloss: I am not very mindful / I have a poor memory
observation: Socrates disclaims being able to recall his previous judgment about Gorgias, citing a poor memory in the immediate present.
textual_basis: When Meno asks whether Socrates did not think Gorgias knew, Socrates replies at 71c that he is not very mindful and cannot say in the present moment how it seemed to him then, using memory failure as a dialogical move that deflects the question rather than answering it.
limits: This does not prove Socrates genuinely forgot; it only records the textual presence of a memory disavowal as an explicit dialogical device. The passage does not resolve whether the disavowal is sincere or ironic.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_meno_0045
source_work: Meno
stephanus_span: 71c-71d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/meno.txt
  stephanus_span: 71c-71d
  start_marker: 71c
  end_marker: 71d
  start_char: 1748
  end_char: 2501
  text_sha256: 4ec9f960f76c79d2a6f2120cba7ee69021a42ab8f4c1923eb9fae6d751f1cc1a
greek_terms:
  - εὐτυχέστατον ψεῦσμα
english_gloss: the most fortunate falsehood
observation: Socrates characterizes his own prior claim — that he has never met anyone who knows what virtue is — as the most fortunate falsehood he would have told if Meno turns out to know.
textual_basis: At 71d Socrates says he will have told a most fortunate falsehood if Meno and Gorgias are shown to know, framing his ignorance-claim as a potentially happy lie contingent on Meno's response. This locates the profession of ignorance within a conditional structure that explicitly entertains its own undoing.
limits: This does not prove that Socrates actually knows what virtue is. It records only the textual move in which Socrates labels his ignorance-claim as a potential falsehood whose value depends on the interlocutor's performance.
review_status: rejected
```
